Question: Find federal datasets about water quality and inspect one.

Expected: Search and inspect; cite publisher and source; no invented observations.

Agent answer

Federal water-quality datasets found

I searched Data.gov for “water quality”, restricted to federal publishers, and reviewed four promising records from the first results page. This is a targeted sample, not an exhaustive inventory.

Dataset Federal publisher Coverage and advertised formats
Water Quality data CP EPA, Office of Research and Development Groundwater and surface-water samples at a permeable reactive barrier; Excel. Sampling dates and location not specified in the inspected metadata.
Water Quality and Macroinvertebrate Data Northern Alaska, 2007 National Park Service Northern Alaska, May 1, 2007; CSV and PDF.
Water Quality and Fish Data—Wrangell–St. Elias and Denali, Alaska, 2003 National Park Service Description gives May 21–September 8, 2003; CSV data, metadata CSV, and PDF report. The structured date field is less specific and lists January 1, 2003.
Water-quality stressors and coral reef health: systematic evidence map EPA, Office of Research and Development Research synthesis covering water-quality stressors and reef condition; Excel. Observation period and geographic extent unspecified.

Inspected: EPA’s Water Quality data CP

  • Contents: Field and laboratory data from groundwater monitoring wells and surface water at a field-scale permeable reactive barrier.
  • Research context: Associated with a 2025 publication on long-term nitrate and ammonium remediation at a livestock feeding operation. This describes the study’s subject, not a verified finding from the data.
  • Advertised file: Water Quality Data CP.xlsx.
  • Access and terms: Cataloged as public, with an EPA ScienceHub license.
  • Missing details: No data dictionary, sampling period, precise location, file size, or column-level schema was supplied in the inspected record.
  • Identifier: DOI 10.23719/1531692.

Limitations: I inspected catalog metadata, not the spreadsheet itself. Download availability and file contents remain unverified, and none of these records has a reviewed connection for retrieving observations here. The next step is to review the selected file and its documentation for variable definitions, units, sampling dates, and quality flags. Across the other records, no explicit data dictionary was exposed; the Alaska 2003 record does advertise a separate metadata file.

D2 run 1 · 22.0 s · discovery

auto: pass

Question: Find housing affordability datasets. Explain which appear useful and what still needs checking.

Expected: Discover across a second topic; distinguish relevance from compatibility.

Agent answer

CHAS looks like the best starting point for local housing-needs analysis; HADS looks useful for historical affordability research. I found three promising HUD records, but none is currently verified for retrieving observations here.

1. Comprehensive Housing Affordability Strategy (CHAS)

HUD CHAS portal · Catalog identifier: HUD044

  • Why useful: Custom Census tabulations describing housing problems and needs, particularly among low-income households. A promising starting point for local housing planning and cost-burden research.
  • Availability: The catalog advertises a “CHAS Query Tool,” labeled as an API. That label does not establish a working programmatic endpoint.
  • Still needs checking: Available observation periods and geographic levels; exact cost-burden definitions and denominators; table documentation and data dictionary; access requirements and reuse terms. These are not established by the inspected record.

2. ACS 5-Year CHAS Estimate Data by County

HUD county dataset · ArcGIS item: dbcc9ba4a4144a6d823b4d429d2b97c6

  • Why useful: The most concrete candidate for county-level maps and comparisons of low-income housing needs. The description identifies household counts by housing problems and income categories.
  • Period: 2016–2020 ACS, explicitly stated in the description—not current conditions. A later metadata update does not change that observation period.
  • Formats advertised: CSV, Excel, ArcGIS REST, GeoJSON, GeoPackage, and other GIS formats.
  • Still needs checking: Exact county coverage, field definitions, missing-value codes, geographic identifiers, and whether a newer release exists. A dictionary is mentioned, but its contents were not inspected.
  • Terms: Marked public, with HUD disclaimers concerning accuracy and liability; full applicable terms still require review.

3. Housing Affordability Data System (HADS)

HUD HADS page · Catalog identifier: HUD031

  • Why useful: Historical housing-unit affordability and owner/renter cost-burden analysis, derived from the American Housing Survey.
  • Periods/access: The description references national surveys from 1985 onward and metro surveys from 2002 onward. It says 2013 is the latest public-use file, with 2015 onward restricted to internal-use files; confirm this access statement with HUD.
  • Still needs checking: Actual available survey years and metro coverage, file formats, documentation, measure definitions, and access/reuse terms.

Recommended next step: Choose a geography and period, then inspect the relevant CHAS dictionary and a small data sample before committing to analysis.

Search scope: One Data.gov keyword-results page for “housing affordability,” with the three HUD candidates inspected in detail, plus the reviewed observation registry. This is a targeted shortlist, not an exhaustive inventory. Advertised download/API links were not fetched or validated.

 "datasets": [

   "title": "Housing Affordability Data System (HADS)",

  "description": "Comprehensive Housing Affordability Strategy (CHAS) data documenting the extent of housing problems and housing needs, particularly for low income households, at the County level. This is estimated by the number of households that have certain housing problems and have income low enough to qualify for HUD\u2019s programs (primarily 30, 50, and 80 percent of median income). The U.S. Department of Housing and Urban Development (HUD) periodically receives \"custom tabulations\" of Census data from the U.S. Census Bureau that are largely not available through standard Census products. These datasets, known as \"CHAS\" (Comprehensive Housing Affordability Strategy) data, demonstrate the extent of housing problems and housing needs, particularly for low income households. The primary purpose of CHAS data is to demonstrate the number of households in need of housing assistance. This is estimated by the number of households that have certain housing problems and have income low enough to qualify for HUD\u2019s programs (primarily 30, 50, and 80 percent of median income). CHAS data provides counts of the numbers of households that fit these HUD-specified characteristics in a variety of geographic areas. In addition to estimating low-income housing needs, CHAS data contributes to a more comprehensive market analysis by documenting issues like lead paint risks, \"affordability mismatch,\" and the interaction of affordability with variables like age of homes, number of bedrooms, and type of building. This dataset is a special tabulation of the 2016-2020 American Community Survey (ACS) and reflects conditions over that time period. The dataset uses custom HUD Area Median Family Income (HAMFI) figures calculated by HUD PDR staff based on 2016-2020 ACS income data. CHAS datasets are used by Federal, State, and Local governments to plan how to spend, and distribute HUD program funds. To learn more about the Comprehensive Housing Affordability Strategy (CHAS), visit: https://www.huduser.gov/portal/datasets/cp.html, for questions about the spatial attribution of this dataset, please reach out to us at GISHelpdesk@hud.gov.    "description": "The Housing Affordability Data System (HADS) is a set of files derived from the 1985 and later national American Housing Survey (AHS) and the 2002 and later Metro AHS. This system categorizes housing units by affordability and households by income, with respect to the Adjusted Median Income, Fair Market Rent (FMR), and poverty income. It also includes housing cost burden for owner and renter households. These files have been the basis for the worst case needs tables since 2001. The data files are available for public use, since they were derived from AHS public use files and the published income limits and FMRs. These dataset give the community of housing analysts the opportunity to use a consistent set of affordability measures. The most recent year HADS is available as a Public Use File (PUF) is 2013. For 2015 and beyond, HADS is only available as an IUF and can no longer be released on a PUF. Those seeking access to more recent data should reach to the listed point of contact.",

   "title": "ACS 5YR CHAS Estimate Data by County",
   "description": "Comprehensive Housing Affordability Strategy (CHAS) data documenting the extent of housing problems and housing needs, particularly for low income households, at the County level. This is estimated by the number of households that have certain housing problems and have income low enough to qualify for HUD\u2019s programs (primarily 30, 50, and 80 percent of median income). The U.S. Department of Housing and Urban Development (HUD) periodically receives \"custom tabulations\" of Census data from the U.S. Census Bureau that are largely not available through standard Census products. These datasets, known as \"CHAS\" (Comprehensive Housing Affordability Strategy) data, demonstrate the extent of housing problems and housing needs, particularly for low income households. The primary purpose of CHAS data is to demonstrate the number of households in need of housing assistance. This is estimated by the number of households that have certain housing problems and have income low enough to qualify for HUD\u2019s programs (primarily 30, 50, and 80 percent of median income). CHAS data provides counts of the numbers of households that fit these HUD-specified characteristics in a variety of geographic areas. In addition to estimating low-income housing needs, CHAS data contributes to a more comprehensive market analysis by documenting issues like lead paint risks, \"affordability mismatch,\" and the interaction of affordability with variables like age of homes, number of bedrooms, and type of building. This dataset is a special tabulation of the 2016-2020 American Community Survey (ACS) and reflects conditions over that time period. The dataset uses custom HUD Area Median Family Income (HAMFI) figures calculated by HUD PDR staff based on 2016-2020 ACS income data. CHAS datasets are used by Federal, State, and Local governments to plan how to spend, and distribute HUD program funds. To learn more about the Comprehensive Housing Affordability Strategy (CHAS), visit: https://www.huduser.gov/portal/datasets/cp.html, for questions about the spatial attribution of this dataset, please reach out to us at GISHelpdesk@hud.gov. To learn more about the American Community Survey (ACS), and associated datasets visit: https://www.census.gov/programs-surveys/acs Data Dictionary: DD_ACS 5-Year CHAS Estimate Data by County Date of Coverage: 2016-2020",

Agent answer

Used the catalog’s Maryland location filter (location_id: "21") with intersection matching (spatial_within: false), rather than requiring datasets to lie entirely within Maryland.

Dataset Geospatial content and coverage Formats and access
GIS Features of the Geospatial Fabric for National Hydrologic Modeling — USGS. Identifier: USGS_581a162ee4b0bb36a4ca2e29 GIS vector features covering the lower 48 states, Hawaii, and Puerto Rico: region outlines, points of interest, routing networks, and hydrologic response units. Broad coverage includes Maryland. Catalog lists an access page and XML metadata; the actual GIS package format was not verified. Metadata marks access public but supplies no explicit license.
Inventory of Owned and Leased Properties — GSA. Identifier: GSA-4495 Nationwide property inventory with addresses and latitude/longitude. The description says coordinates are available only through CSV export. Listed distributions include buildings and leases in XLSX, plus XML metadata; no CSV distribution was listed in the inspected record. License: CC0.

Coverage and caveats: I reviewed the first page of an intersection-filtered search and a second search narrowed with “geospatial”; this is not an exhaustive inventory. Catalog location matching uses metadata extents, so a national dataset can match Maryland without confirming individual Maryland features.

Neither record specifies an observation period or provides a separate data dictionary in the inspected metadata. Metadata modification dates are not observation dates. Both are discovery results: download availability and schemas remain unverified, and neither can return observations through the reviewed data connections here.

For GIS use, the next step is to inspect the USGS package’s regional files or verify the GSA coordinate export, then select or clip features to Maryland.

A graphic showing the boundaries for all Regions is provided at http://dx.doi.org/doi:10.5066/F7542KMD. These Regions are identical to those used to organize the NHDPlus v.1 dataset (US EPA and US Geological Survey, 2005). Although the GF Feature data set has been derived from NHDPlus v.1, it is an entirely new data set that has been designed to generically support regional and national scale applications of hydrologic models. Definition of each type of feature class and its derivation is provided within the &lt;dataqual&gt;&lt;lineage&gt; section of this metadata document. The first &lt;procstep&gt; entry in that section provides an overview of the delineation process, with each subsequent &lt;procstep&gt; corresponding to one of the four types of feature. These &lt;procstep&gt; entries describe the derivation of feature types in the order in which they are created. Minimal attribution (feature size, location, and routing connectivity) is provided for the feature classes within the GF Feature data set. More extensive feature attribution is published separately as individual tables of attributes(for example, http://dx.doi.org/doi:10.5066/F7RX9937) or via entire configurations of tables engineered to satisfy particular watershed models (for example, http://dx.doi.org/doi:10.5066/F7WM1BF7).",

M1 run 1 · 7.1 s · methodology

auto: pass

Question: Does the unemployment rate use total population as its denominator?

Expected: No; civilian labor force. Inspect registered definition.

Agent answer

No. The unemployment rate uses the civilian labor force, not total population, as its denominator:

[ \text{Unemployment rate}=\frac{\text{Unemployed people}}{\text{Civilian labor force}}\times100 ]

The labor force consists of employed and unemployed people—not everyone in the population. People outside the labor force are excluded.

Source: the registered definition for unemployment_rate in md_county_population_unemployment, based on Bureau of Labor Statistics LAUS data (catalog ID: DOL-BLS-3060-01).

M2 run 1 · 7.1 s · methodology

auto: warn

Question: Can we treat a catalog harvest date as the observation year?

Expected: No; separate harvest, release and observation dates.

Agent answer

No. A catalog harvest date is not an observation year. It records when metadata was collected or refreshed in the catalog—not when the underlying phenomenon was measured.

Keep these dates separate:

  • Observation period: when the measurements apply.
  • Publication or update date: when the publisher released or revised the data.
  • Harvest date: when the catalog ingested or refreshed the metadata.

For example, a dataset harvested in 2025 could contain observations for 2010–2020.

Use the dataset’s documented time fields, temporal coverage, or data dictionary to identify observation years. If those are missing or ambiguous, report the observation period as unknown rather than substituting the harvest year.

M3 run 1 · 9.1 s · methodology

auto: pass

Question: Can county unemployment rates be averaged to get the state rate?

Expected: No unweighted average; aggregate unemployed and labor-force counts.

Agent answer

Not with a simple average. County unemployment rates must be weighted by each county’s civilian labor force—not its total population.

The appropriate calculation is:

[ \text{State unemployment rate} =100\times\frac{\sum_{\text{counties}}\text{unemployed persons}} {\sum_{\text{counties}}\text{civilian labor force}} ]

This is equivalent to a labor-force-weighted average of county rates, provided the rates are unrounded. A simple average gives a small county the same influence as a large one.

Use all counties and county equivalents, matching the same period and seasonal-adjustment status. Prefer the underlying unemployed and labor-force counts over rounded published rates. If you need the official state rate, use the published state series rather than assuming an aggregation will reproduce it exactly.

Source: The registered definition for md_county_population_unemployment, measure unemployment_rate, defines the denominator as the civilian labor force and explicitly says to sum unemployed and labor-force counts rather than average county rates. Its source is BLS Local Area Unemployment Statistics, catalog identifier DOL-BLS-3060-01.

citationspassCites registered measure and BLS source with catalog identifier.
limitationspassNotes period/seasonal consistency and recommends the official state series.

A1 run 1 · 14.1 s · analysis

auto: pass

Question: Which Maryland counties grew in population while unemployment fell from 2022 to 2024?

Expected: Assess, compare increase/decrease, retrieve evidence; exact reference rows required.

Agent answer

Worcester County was the only Maryland county where population grew while the annual-average unemployment rate fell from 2022 to 2024.

County Population, 2022 Population, 2024 Population change Unemployment, 2022 Unemployment, 2024 Rate change
Worcester 54,004 54,337 +0.62% 4.7% 4.4% −0.3 percentage points

The comparison covered all 24 Maryland county-level jurisdictions, including Baltimore city, with no missing endpoint data.

Sources: Census county population estimates, Vintage 2024 and BLS Local Area Unemployment Statistics. Analysis evidence ID: be2baff021504f26b298d36cb384c23c.

“Unemployment fell” here means the unemployment rate, not the number unemployed. Population is a July 1 estimate; unemployment is an annual average, not seasonally adjusted. This compares endpoints, not a continuous trend or causal relationship.

Your score

A2 run 1 · 13.0 s · analysis

auto: pass

Question: Compare Baltimore city's population and unemployment in 2022 and 2024.

Expected: County FIPS 24510; percent population change and percentage-point unemployment change.

Agent answer

Baltimore city’s population declined slightly from 2022 to 2024, while its unemployment rate increased.

Measure 2022 2024 Change
Resident population 570,663 568,271 −0.42%
Annual-average unemployment rate 3.8% 4.3% +0.5 percentage points

These figures cover Baltimore city (FIPS 24510), not Baltimore County.

  • Population is the July 1 estimate for each year, using the Census Bureau’s same Vintage 2024 estimates.
  • Unemployment is the annual-average, not-seasonally-adjusted rate: unemployed people divided by the civilian labor force—not the total population.

This is a comparison of two endpoints, not evidence of a continuous trend or a causal relationship.

Sources: Census population estimates, Vintage 2024; BLS Local Area Unemployment Statistics (identifier: DOL-BLS-3060-01).
Analysis evidence: a9972798ff3649b4830b3d697f6b17de.

auto: pass

Question: Rank Maryland counties by population growth from 2023 to 2024.

Expected: Registered comparison sorted by population_change; cite same-vintage population estimates.

Agent answer

Maryland counties ranked by population growth, 2023–2024

Queen Anne’s County grew fastest, at 1.91%, followed by Frederick County at 1.76%. The ranking below uses percentage growth, not the number of residents added.

Population figures are July 1 estimates from the Census Bureau’s Vintage 2024 series. Baltimore city is included as a county equivalent.

Rank County / county equivalent 2023 population 2024 population Growth
1 Queen Anne’s 52,682 53,688 1.91%
2 Frederick 294,154 299,317 1.76%
3 Charles 172,268 174,478 1.28%
4 Caroline 33,838 34,248 1.21%
5 Montgomery 1,069,397 1,082,273 1.20%
6 Prince George’s 956,533 966,629 1.06%
7 St. Mary’s 115,479 116,469 0.86%
8 Somerset 25,044 25,241 0.79%
9 Washington 156,116 157,228 0.71%
10 Wicomico 105,585 106,329 0.70%
11 Howard 337,341 339,668 0.69%
12 Dorchester 32,922 33,138 0.66%
13 Kent 19,441 19,557 0.60%
14 Cecil 105,676 106,305 0.60%
15 Talbot 38,067 38,244 0.46%
16 Baltimore County 848,676 852,425 0.44%
17 Anne Arundel 599,965 602,350 0.40%
18 Harford 264,771 265,514 0.28%
19 Carroll 176,735 177,108 0.21%
20 Worcester 54,229 54,337 0.20%
21 Baltimore city 567,517 568,271 0.13%
22 Calvert 94,828 94,913 0.09%
23 Allegany 67,296 67,097 −0.30%
24 Garrett 28,502 28,393 −0.38%

Overall: 22 county-level jurisdictions increased in population and two decreased.

Growth is defined as 100 × (2024 population − 2023 population) / 2023 population. Rankings use unrounded values; these are one-year changes, not long-term trends.

Source: U.S. Census Bureau, County Population Estimates, Vintage 2024 (CSV). Analysis evidence ID: bce16fe6e1ef414aaf8c651615c530bf.

auto: pass

Question: Compare California county population and annual-average unemployment rate changes from 2020 to 2024.

Expected: Explicit unsupported geography/period; discovery may still help.

Agent answer

I can’t verify California’s county-by-county changes for 2020–2024 with the data accessible here. The reviewed population-and-unemployment data cover Maryland for 2022–2024—not California—so substituting them would give the wrong comparison.

Catalog searches for California county population and unemployment, and for 2024 county population estimates, did not establish a usable source covering both requested endpoints. The California Department of Public Health’s Unemployment record (ID 93962fda-b3c2-4344-a77a-293cbf43ab88) advertises an unemployment workbook, but it is labeled 2004–2013, not 2020–2024. Its contents and download access have not been verified.

The concrete next step is to obtain and validate matching California county observations from:

  • Census county population estimates: 2020 and 2024 from the same estimate vintage.
  • BLS Local Area Unemployment Statistics: annual-average county unemployment rates for 2020 and 2024.

The resulting comparison should distinguish population percent change from unemployment-rate change in percentage points, and describe endpoint changes—not a continuous trend or a causal relationship. I cannot yet report which counties grew, lost population, or had the largest unemployment-rate declines without verified observations.

Your score

U3 run 1 · 19.1 s · unsupported

auto: pass

Question: Did population growth cause Maryland unemployment to fall?

Expected: Decline causal conclusion; descriptive comparison alone cannot establish it.

Agent answer

The verified data do not establish that population growth caused Maryland unemployment to fall. That is not proof of no effect—just that a causal conclusion is unsupported.

Since you did not specify a period, I checked 2022–2024, the available reviewed county-level comparison:

  • Population increased in 21 of Maryland’s 24 county-level jurisdictions, including Baltimore city.
  • Unemployment rates increased in 18, fell in 1, and were unchanged in 5.
  • 15 jurisdictions had both population growth and a higher unemployment rate.

These are county direction counts, not a statewide unemployment-rate change. They do not establish a statewide decline or explain its cause. Sources: Census population estimates, Vintage 2024 and BLS Maryland unemployment data, catalog ID DOL-BLS-3060-01. Analysis evidence: 2aa4fd2bdf1347929958420b69344f70.

The registered definition also matters: the unemployment rate is unemployed people divided by the civilian labor force—not total population. Population growth therefore does not mechanically lower it. These observations compare July 1 population estimates with annual-average unemployment rates; endpoint changes are neither a trend estimate nor a causal test.

Supplementary catalog searches did not establish another verified Maryland source suitable for answering the causal question.

Which years did you have in mind? The next step is to verify the statewide decline over that period before investigating its causes.
